mcode-m3.1-flash/03-infra-management/app.py

"""Minimal JSON API for the deployment.

This module exists so the health checks in compose.yaml probe something real
instead of a guess. It is deliberately small:

  GET /health  liveness  - "the process is up and serving". No dependency
                          calls, so a database outage never triggers a
                          restart loop.
  GET /ready   readiness - "dependencies are usable". Backs the proxy's
                          `depends_on: condition: service_healthy`, so nginx
                          is only declared healthy once the API can actually
                          serve traffic.

Replace the `/` body with the real application; keep the two health
endpoints and their semantics (liveness cheap, readiness checks deps).
"""

import os
import time

# Only the web framework is imported at startup. The database and cache
# drivers are imported inside the readiness checks, so a broken or missing
# driver shows up as /ready returning 503 rather than the process failing to
# boot - which is what keeps /health honest as a liveness signal.
from flask import Flask, jsonify
from werkzeug.middleware.proxy_fix import ProxyFix

app = Flask(__name__)

# Trust exactly one reverse proxy hop (nginx), so request.remote_addr and
# url_scheme are the real client values instead of the proxy's. The X-Forwarded-*
# headers nginx sets are only honoured from that hop.
app.wsgi_app = ProxyFix(
    app.wsgi_app,
    x_for=1,
    x_proto=1,
    x_host=1,
    x_port=0,
    x_prefix=0,
)

# Seconds. Kept short so a hung dependency fails the probe instead of
# stalling it; the probe is retried by the healthcheck interval.
DEPENDENCY_TIMEOUT = float(os.environ.get("DEPENDENCY_TIMEOUT", "2"))


def _database_url() -> str:
    """Build a DSN from discrete parts, so passwords need no URL escaping.

    A password containing '@', ':' or '/' breaks a hand-written
    postgres:// URL; libpq keyword arguments do not have that failure mode.
    An explicit DATABASE_URL still wins if it is set.
    """
    url = os.environ.get("DATABASE_URL")
    if url:
        return url
    return " ".join(
        [
            f"host={os.environ.get('POSTGRES_HOST', 'db')}",
            f"port={os.environ.get('POSTGRES_PORT', '5432')}",
            f"user={os.environ.get('POSTGRES_USER', 'app')}",
            f"password={os.environ.get('POSTGRES_PASSWORD', '')}",
            f"dbname={os.environ.get('POSTGRES_DB', 'app')}",
            "connect_timeout=3",
        ]
    )


def _redis_url() -> str:
    url = os.environ.get("REDIS_URL")
    if url:
        return url
    host = os.environ.get("REDIS_HOST", "cache")
    port = os.environ.get("REDIS_PORT", "6379")
    password = os.environ.get("REDIS_PASSWORD", "")
    auth = f":{password}@" if password else ""
    return f"redis://{auth}{host}:{port}/0"


def check_postgres() -> tuple[bool, str]:
    started = time.monotonic()
    conn = None
    try:
        import psycopg2

        conn = psycopg2.connect(_database_url(), connect_timeout=3)
        with conn.cursor() as cur:
            cur.execute("SELECT 1")
            cur.fetchone()
        return True, f"{(time.monotonic() - started) * 1000:.1f}ms"
    except Exception as exc:  # noqa: BLE001 - reported, never fatal to serving
        return False, type(exc).__name__
    finally:
        # psycopg2's connection context manager only ends the transaction, it
        # does not close the socket, so closing is explicit.
        if conn is not None:
            conn.close()


def check_redis() -> tuple[bool, str]:
    started = time.monotonic()
    client = None
    try:
        import redis

        client = redis.Redis.from_url(
            _redis_url(),
            socket_timeout=DEPENDENCY_TIMEOUT,
            socket_connect_timeout=DEPENDENCY_TIMEOUT,
        )
        client.ping()
        return True, f"{(time.monotonic() - started) * 1000:.1f}ms"
    except Exception as exc:  # noqa: BLE001
        return False, type(exc).__name__
    finally:
        # The client owns a connection pool; release it per probe so a
        # frequent probe cannot accumulate idle sockets in the worker.
        if client is not None:
            client.close()


@app.get("/health")
def health():
    """Liveness: process is up. Must not touch dependencies."""
    return jsonify(status="ok"), 200


@app.get("/ready")
def ready():
    """Readiness: dependencies are usable."""
    db_ok, db_detail = check_postgres()
    cache_ok, cache_detail = check_redis()
    healthy = db_ok and cache_ok
    return (
        jsonify(
            status="ok" if healthy else "degraded",
            checks={"postgres": db_ok, "redis": cache_ok},
            details={"postgres": db_detail, "redis": cache_detail},
        ),
        200 if healthy else 503,
    )


@app.get("/")
def index():
    return jsonify(service=os.environ.get("SERVICE_NAME", "api"), status="ok"), 200


if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Local debugging only. Compose runs gunicorn (see Dockerfile CMD).
    app.run(host="127.0.0.1", port=8000, debug=False)

mcode-m3.1-flash/03-infra-management/compose.yaml

# Deployment for a small JSON API behind nginx, with Postgres and Redis.
#
# Scope note: this is plain Docker Compose, not an orchestrator. It gives you
# health-gated startup, restart policies, log rotation, resource ceilings and
# two network tiers - which is what a single-host deployment needs. It does NOT
# give you multi-host failover, rolling updates, secret rotation or
# autoscaling. Those need a real orchestrator; see RUNBOOK.md "Limits".
#
# Required setup:  cp .env.example .env   (then edit the passwords)
# Validate:       docker compose config --quiet
# Start:          docker compose up -d --build
#
# Every ${VAR} below is read from .env by Compose before the stack starts, so
# no credential is ever committed here. Nothing in this file publishes a
# database or cache port to the host.

name: small-api

# Uniform log rotation. Without this the json-file driver grows without bound
# and fills the disk - the default Docker behaviour is a real outage cause.
x-logging: &default-logging
  driver: json-file
  options:
    max-size: "10m"
    max-file: "3"

services:
  # ---------------------------------------------------------------- edge ----
  proxy:
    image: nginx:stable-alpine
    # Only the edge is reachable from outside. Bound via .env so a laptop can
    # use 127.0.0.1:8080 while a real host listens on 0.0.0.0:80.
    ports:
      - "${HTTP_BIND:-0.0.0.0}:${HTTP_PORT:-80}:80"
    volumes:
      # Read-only: nginx must never be able to rewrite its own config.
      - ./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/nginx.conf:ro
    depends_on:
      api:
        # Wait for the API to pass readiness, not merely to be started.
        condition: service_healthy
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "wget -qO- http://127.0.0.1/healthz >/dev/null 2>&1 || exit 1"]
      interval: 15s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 5s
    networks:
      - frontend
    restart: unless-stopped
    stop_grace_period: 10s
    logging: *default-logging
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    # A ceiling, not a reservation. Compose enforces these for a single
    # container; `deploy.resources` would be ignored outside Swarm.
    mem_limit: 256m
    cpus: 1.0
    pids_limit: 100

  # ---------------------------------------------------------------- api ----
  api:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
    # No `ports:`. The API is reachable only from the proxy, over `frontend`.
    environment:
      SERVICE_NAME: api
      # Discrete parts rather than a postgres:// URL: a password containing
      # '@', ':' or '/' would need percent-encoding inside a URL and would
      # fail to parse. app.py builds a libpq DSN from these.
      POSTGRES_HOST: db
      POSTGRES_PORT: "5432"
      POSTGRES_USER: ${POSTGRES_USER:?set POSTGRES_USER in .env}
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}
      POSTGRES_DB: ${POSTGRES_DB:?set POSTGRES_DB in .env}
      REDIS_HOST: cache
      REDIS_PORT: "6379"
      REDIS_PASSWORD: ${REDIS_PASSWORD:?set REDIS_PASSWORD in .env}
      # Capacity tuning, appended to the gunicorn args baked into the image.
      GUNICORN_CMD_ARGS: >-
        --workers=${API_WORKERS:-2}
        --threads=${API_THREADS:-4}
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      cache:
        condition: service_healthy
    healthcheck:
      # Readiness, not liveness: nginx should not be marked healthy while the
      # API cannot reach its dependencies. A failing probe does not restart
      # the container (restart is driven by the restart policy), so a database
      # outage cannot turn into a restart loop.
      test:
        - CMD
        - python
        - -c
        - |
          import sys, urllib.request
          try:
              sys.exit(0 if urllib.request.urlopen("http://127.0.0.1:8000/ready", timeout=3).status == 200 else 1)
          except Exception:
              sys.exit(1)
      interval: 15s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 4
      start_period: 20s
    networks:
      - frontend
      - backend
    restart: unless-stopped
    # Long enough for gunicorn to finish in-flight requests on SIGTERM.
    stop_grace_period: 35s
    read_only: true
    # read_only blocks the only path gunicorn needs for temp files.
    tmpfs:
      - /tmp:size=64m,mode=1777
    logging: *default-logging
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    mem_limit: 512m
    cpus: 1.5
    pids_limit: 200

  # ----------------------------------------------------------------- db ----
  db:
    # Major version pinned; ':latest' means a major upgrade can land silently
    # on the next `up` and can refuse to start against an existing data dir.
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: ${POSTGRES_USER:?set POSTGRES_USER in .env}
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}
      POSTGRES_DB: ${POSTGRES_DB:?set POSTGRES_DB in .env}
      # Deterministic collation regardless of the host's locale.
      POSTGRES_INITDB_ARGS: "--encoding=UTF8 --locale=C"
    # No published port. The only client is the api container, over `backend`.
    # To inspect from a laptop, use `docker compose exec db psql -U $POSTGRES_USER app`.
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    # Postgres wants more shared memory than Docker's 64MB default for
    # parallel queries and large sorts.
    shm_size: 256mb
    command:
      - postgres
      # Fewer than the default 100 connections: gunicorn workers x threads all
      # connect here, and a connection ceiling is cheap protection against one
      # bad client exhausting the server's slots.
      - -c
      - max_connections=100
    healthcheck:
      # $$ escapes so the value is expanded by the shell INSIDE the container
      # from its own environment, rather than being baked in by Compose.
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -q -U \"$$POSTGRES_USER\" -d \"$$POSTGRES_DB\""]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 30s
    networks:
      - backend
    restart: unless-stopped
    # Long enough for a clean shutdown checkpoint, instead of a kill mid-write.
    stop_grace_period: 30s
    logging: *default-logging
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    mem_limit: 1g
    cpus: 2.0
    pids_limit: 200

  # -------------------------------------------------------------- cache ----
  cache:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    # Must be present: the command below and the healthcheck both read it from
    # the container's own environment. Without it redis would start with an
    # empty requirepass and the healthcheck could never pass, which would block
    # the api from ever starting (it waits on service_healthy).
    environment:
      REDIS_PASSWORD: ${REDIS_PASSWORD:?set REDIS_PASSWORD in .env}
    # No published port, and no volume: this is a cache, and a lost cache must
    # be cheap. Persistence is off, so a restart does not block on an RDB load.
    command:
      - sh
      - -c
      - >
        exec redis-server
        --requirepass "$$REDIS_PASSWORD"
        --appendonly no
        --save ""
        --maxmemory 256mb
        --maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru
    healthcheck:
      # REDISCLI_AUTH keeps the password out of the command line, so it does
      # not show up in `ps` inside the container.
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "REDISCLI_AUTH=\"$$REDIS_PASSWORD\" redis-cli ping | grep -q PONG"]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 5
      start_period: 5s
    networks:
      - backend
    restart: unless-stopped
    stop_grace_period: 10s
    logging: *default-logging
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    mem_limit: 384m
    cpus: 1.0
    pids_limit: 100

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- networks --
networks:
  # Edge tier: the proxy, and the API's inbound side.
  frontend:
    driver: bridge
  # Data tier: API, db, cache. `internal: true` means these containers have no
  # route to the outside world - a compromised database cannot call home, and
  # the data stores are not exposed on the host's networks at all. The API is
  # on both tiers so it can still reach the internet if it ever needs to.
  backend:
    driver: bridge
    internal: true

volumes:
  # Named volume, not a bind mount: a bind mount inherits host directory
  # ownership and is easy to back up or destroy by accident.
  pgdata:

mcode-m3.1-flash/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1.7
#
# Image for the small JSON API. Priorities, in order:
#   1. no secrets in the image or in the build context
#   2. run as a non-root user
#   3. production WSGI server, not the Flask dev server
#   4. reproducible installs (pinned requirements, resolved once, at build time)
#
# Build from this directory:  docker compose build api
FROM python:3.13-slim-bookworm

# PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE: no stray .pyc files in the image layer.
# PYTHONUNBUFFERED: logs reach `docker compose logs` immediately.
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_DISABLE_PIP_VERSION_CHECK=1 \
    PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1 \
    VIRTUAL_ENV=/opt/venv \
    PATH=/opt/venv/bin:$PATH

# Fixed uid/gid so bind-mounted files have predictable ownership.
RUN groupadd --system --gid 10001 app \
 && useradd  --system --uid 10001 --gid app --home-dir /app --shell /usr/sbin/nologin app

WORKDIR /app

# Dependencies first: this layer is only invalidated when requirements.txt
# changes, so ordinary code edits do not re-run pip.
COPY requirements.txt ./

# psycopg2-binary ships wheels, so no compiler toolchain is needed in the
# final image. --no-compile keeps image size down. `pip install -r` uses the
# exact == pins, so the resolved dependency set is reproducible.
# (For a stronger guarantee add --require-hashes with a generated
#  requirements.lock; that needs a network resolve and is out of scope here.)
RUN python -m venv "$VIRTUAL_ENV" \
 && pip install --no-cache-dir --upgrade pip \
 && pip install --no-cache-dir --no-compile -r requirements.txt

# Copy only the application source. Never `COPY . .`: the build context
# contains .env, which must stay out of the image (see .dockerignore).
COPY --chown=app:app app.py ./

# Drop to the unprivileged user for the rest of the build and for runtime.
USER 10001:10001

# Not published to the host: nginx reaches the API over the internal network.
EXPOSE 8000

# Runtime tuning lives in two places on purpose:
#   * safety-relevant defaults are pinned below, so the image is correct on its
#     own (e.g. when run with `docker run` or by a different orchestrator);
#   * capacity knobs (workers/threads) are overridden in compose.yaml through
#     GUNICORN_CMD_ARGS, which gunicorn appends to this list, so tuning does
#     not require editing the image or duplicating flags in two places.
# --forwarded-allow-ips must cover the nginx container IP: gunicorn ignores
# X-Forwarded-* from any source outside this list, and ProxyFix is how the app
# learns the real client address and scheme. `*` is acceptable here because the
# API port is never published to the host, so only a container on the internal
# network can reach it. Narrow it to a fixed proxy IP if that changes.
STOPSIGNAL SIGTERM
CMD ["gunicorn", \
     "--bind=0.0.0.0:8000", \
     "--worker-class=gthread", \
     "--timeout=30", \
     "--graceful-timeout=30", \
     "--keep-alive=5", \
     "--max-requests=1000", \
     "--max-requests-jitter=100", \
     "--access-logfile=-", \
     "--error-logfile=-", \
     "--capture-output", \
     "--forwarded-allow-ips=*", \
     "app:app"]

mcode-m3.1-flash/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

# Full nginx config for the edge of the small API. Mounted read-only at
# /etc/nginx/nginx.conf (see compose.yaml), replacing the image's default.
#
# Tuned for a JSON API: small bodies, fast responses, no HTML, no
# long-polling. If you add file uploads or SSE, revisit client_max_body_size
# and proxy_read_timeout - they are the two settings that will bite you.

user  nginx;
# One worker per CPU, bounded by Docker's cpus limit for this container.
worker_processes  auto;

error_log  /var/log/nginx/error.log warn;
pid        /var/run/nginx.pid;

events {
    worker_connections  1024;
}

http {
    include       /etc/nginx/mime.types;
    default_type  application/json;

    # Do not advertise the nginx version in responses and in the Server header.
    server_tokens off;

    # ---- logging ---------------------------------------------------------
    # JSON lines, so log shipping does not need a grok pattern. request_time
    # and upstream_response_time make slow requests obvious; upstream_connect
    # separates "API was slow" from "API was unreachable".
    log_format json_combined escape=json
      '{'
        '"time":"$time_iso8601",'
        '"request_id":"$request_id",'
        '"remote_addr":"$remote_addr",'
        '"method":"$request_method",'
        '"uri":"$uri",'
        '"status":$status,'
        '"bytes_sent":$body_bytes_sent,'
        '"request_time":$request_time,'
        '"upstream_addr":"$upstream_addr",'
        '"upstream_status":"$upstream_status",'
        '"upstream_connect_time":"$upstream_connect_time",'
        '"upstream_response_time":"$upstream_response_time",'
        '"user_agent":"$http_user_agent"'
      '}';

    access_log  /var/log/nginx/access.log  json_combined buffer=32k  flush=5s;

    # ---- transport -------------------------------------------------------
    sendfile        on;
    tcp_nopush      on;
    tcp_nodelay     on;
    server_names_hash_bucket_size 128;

    keepalive_timeout  65;
    # A JSON API request should not take a minute to arrive.
    client_body_timeout   15s;
    client_header_timeout 15s;
    send_timeout          30s;
    # Free the client's connection when it is too slow to send a body, rather
    # than holding it open.
    reset_timedout_connection on;

    # 1 MB is generous for a JSON body. Raise deliberately if you add uploads.
    client_max_body_size 1m;
    client_body_buffer_size 16k;

    # ---- compression -----------------------------------------------------
    gzip              on;
    gzip_vary         on;
    gzip_proxied      any;
    gzip_comp_level   5;
    gzip_min_length   1024;
    gzip_types        application/json application/javascript text/plain text/css;

    # ---- upstream DNS ----------------------------------------------------
    # Docker's embedded DNS server. With `proxy_pass http://api:8000` nginx
    # resolves "api" once at startup and caches it forever, so after the api
    # container is recreated with a new IP every request fails with 502 until
    # nginx is reloaded. Using a variable in proxy_pass makes nginx re-resolve
    # per request using the TTL below.
    resolver  127.0.0.11 ipv6=off valid=10s;
    resolver_timeout 5s;

    server {
        listen      80 default_server;
        listen      [::]:80 default_server;
        server_name _;

        # Rejecting unexpected Host headers is worth doing on a real
        # deployment, by setting server_name to the real name and adding a
        # catch-all server that returns 444. Left permissive here so that
        # localhost, 127.0.0.1 and container IPs all work out of the box.

        # Proxy's own liveness, used by the container healthcheck. Probing
        # nginx rather than the API is intentional: nginx being healthy must
        # not depend on the API, or a database outage would mark the edge
        # unhealthy and trigger a pointless restart loop. The api -> proxy
        # dependency is health-gated in compose.yaml instead.
        location = /healthz {
            access_log off;
            default_type text/plain;
            return 200 "ok\n";
        }

        location / {
            set $api_upstream api:8000;

            # Variable form: enables resolver-based re-resolution (see above).
            # No URI part, so the original request URI is passed through
            # unchanged.
            proxy_pass http://$api_upstream;

            proxy_http_version 1.1;
            # Empty Connection header keeps the upstream connection alive for
            # reuse; without it nginx sends "Connection: close".
            proxy_set_header Connection        "";

            # ---- forwarded headers ----
            # gunicorn + ProxyFix rebuild the client address and scheme from
            # these, so a missing one shows up as wrong IPs and http:// URLs in
            # logs and redirects.
            proxy_set_header Host              $host;
            proxy_set_header X-Real-IP         $remote_addr;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For   $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Host  $host;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Port  $server_port;
            # Correlate proxy and application logs; also pass through one the
            # client supplied.
            proxy_set_header X-Request-ID      $request_id;

            # ---- timeouts ----
            # Fail fast when the API is down instead of pinning a worker
            # indefinitely. proxy_read_timeout must exceed the API's own
            # gunicorn --timeout (30s) or nginx will cut off slow-but-fine
            # requests.
            proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
            proxy_send_timeout    35s;
            proxy_read_timeout    35s;

            # Small responses; do not buffer a large temp file to disk.
            proxy_buffering       on;
            proxy_buffer_size     8k;
            proxy_buffers         8 8k;
            proxy_busy_buffers_size 16k;

            # Return the API's own error body rather than an HTML error page:
            # clients of a JSON API should always get JSON back, including for
            # gateway errors.
            proxy_intercept_errors off;
        }
    }
}

mcode-m3.1-flash/03-infra-management/requirements.txt

Flask==3.1.2
gunicorn==23.0.0
psycopg2-binary==2.9.10
redis==6.4.0

Evaluation 8 / 10 graded blind as submission M

Good, practical hardening: read_only proxy with all temp paths moved to tmpfs, variable proxy_pass, a tagged API image with an APP_VERSION rollback, and blank secrets that fail closed. Weaker points: the publish is on all interfaces, Redis uses -a and puts its password in argv with no memory cap, and the backup commands hard-code app/app.

Strengths

  • proxy read_only with tmpfs /var/cache/nginx, /var/run and /tmp; nginx.conf relocates all five temp paths
  • Blank passwords in .env.example make config fail; api read_only with cap_drop ALL
  • Release and rollback via APP_VERSION with retained images; migration caveat

Weaknesses

  • Backup and restore use -U app -d app while the comment claims .env values apply
  • Redis password in argv plus redis-cli -a; no maxmemory; AOF on a cache
  • Bind is 0.0.0.0:80; pg_isready over the socket
  • RESPONSE says '(See repository session for command output.)' instead of results
Evidence the grader checked
  • Rendered: proxy ro=True tmpfs [/var/cache/nginx,/var/run,/tmp]
  • RUNBOOK backup block pg_dump -U app -d app -Fc

Objective checks

Files

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

Response — highest-risk original problems and verification

Highest-risk problems in the supplied configuration

  1. Weak hardcoded credentials on a published database port. compose.yaml set POSTGRES_PASSWORD: password (user admin), inlined the same secret in the api's DATABASE_URL, and published 5432:5432 to the host — anyone who could reach the host could log in to Postgres with a password that was also sitting in the repo. Redis (6379:6379) was likewise published with no authentication at all, which is effectively remote code execution on the cache host.
  2. API exposed around the proxy, running as root on the dev server. The api published 5000:5000, bypassing nginx entirely, and the Dockerfile ran flask run (single-threaded development server, not built for untrusted traffic) as root with FLASK_ENV=development — one app bug away from a root shell in the container.
  3. Nothing pinned, dependencies not reproducible. python:3.13, nginx:latest, postgres:latest, redis:latest, and pip install flask psycopg2 redis (no versions, ignoring the pinned requirements.txt that was already present, and choosing source-built psycopg2 over the shipped binary). Any rebuild could silently change every layer, including major database upgrades against an existing data directory.
  4. No Postgres persistence. No volume for /var/lib/postgresql/data, so recreating the db container destroyed the database.
  5. Startup raced dependency readiness. List-form depends_on only waits for containers to start, and there were no healthchecks anywhere; the api could crash-loop against a Postgres that was still initialising, and "is it up?" had no machine-readable answer.
  6. Proxy passed no client context and had no bounds. nginx.conf set no Host/X-Real-IP/X-Forwarded-* headers (the app could not see real client IPs or scheme, breaking logging, rate decisions, and secure cookies), and set no timeouts or body-size limit, so slow or oversized requests could tie up API workers indefinitely. It also resolved api once at startup, so a recreated api container left nginx proxying to a dead IP.
  7. Build context leaked into the image. COPY . . with no .dockerignore baked whatever sat beside the Dockerfile — including a real .env — into the image, and copying source before installing dependencies invalidated the dependency layer on every code change.
  8. No operational guard-rails. No restart policy, unbounded container logs (a chatty service could fill the disk), no CPU/memory limits, one implicit flat network (the proxy could reach Postgres directly), and .env.example disagreed with compose.yaml (user app/change-me in the example vs hardcoded admin/password actually used).

What changed (summary)

  • Dockerfile: pinned slim base, deps installed from requirements.txt with --no-cache-dir before source copy, dedicated non-root app user, gunicorn instead of flask run, stdlib HEALTHCHECK on /health, dev-mode env removed. Added .dockerignore (notably excludes .env).
  • compose.yaml: secrets only via .env interpolation with :? fail-fast; no published ports for api/db/cache; Redis requires a password; pinned images; named volumes for Postgres (and Redis AOF); explicit edge/backend networks with backend internal; healthgated depends_on (service_healthy); per-service healthchecks; restart: unless-stopped, bounded json-file logging, CPU/memory limits; read-only root filesystems + tmpfs for proxy/api, cap_drop: ALL and no-new-privileges for the api; APP_VERSION image tagging for rollback. .env.example now matches the variables actually used and ships empty passwords with generation instructions; .gitignore covers .env.
  • nginx.conf: forwarding headers, runtime re-resolution of api via Docker DNS (resolver 127.0.0.11 + variable proxy_pass), connect/send/ read timeouts aligned with gunicorn, client_max_body_size 1m, gzip for JSON, server_tokens off, baseline security headers, and a proxy-local /healthz for its own healthcheck.
  • RUNBOOK.md: startup, health verification, pg_dump/pg_restore backup/restore, secret generation/rotation, tag-based rollback, and the limits of single-host Compose (plus the /health and app:app entrypoint assumptions).

Verification performed

Per the task constraints, no containers were started and no network was used; verification was static only:

  • Read all five supplied files in full and audited them line by line against the task goals before editing.
  • Parsed the rewritten compose.yaml with Python/PyYAML to confirm valid YAML. (See repository session for command output.)
  • Ran docker compose config (client-side render/validation only — pulls and starts nothing) with throwaway POSTGRES_PASSWORD/REDIS_PASSWORD values supplied via the shell environment (no .env file created), and inspected the rendered config to confirm: only the proxy publishes a port; db/cache have none; healthchecks and service_healthy conditions render as intended; both networks and both named volumes are wired to the right services; interpolated DATABASE_URL/REDIS_URL contain no committed secret.
  • Repeated docker compose config without the passwords to confirm Compose fails fast with the :? error instead of rendering empty credentials.
  • Grepped the final files for regression markers: latest, password as a literal value, FLASK_ENV, flask run, published 5432/6379 mappings, and USER/root usage — confirming the original defects are gone and that Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf agree on the api port (5000) and service name (api).
  • requirements.txt already pinned gunicorn==23.0.0 and psycopg2-binary, which the new Dockerfile now actually installs; no dependency changes were needed.

Not verified (out of scope / impossible under the constraints): actually building or booting the stack, runtime behaviour of the healthchecks against the real application (there is no app source in this directory — GET /health and the app:app entrypoint are documented assumptions in RUNBOOK.md), nginx -t against the nginx binary (none installed locally; config was checked by inspection), and that the pinned image tags resolve in a registry (no network). These need a live docker compose up smoke test on a host with registry access before production use.

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

Runbook — Python API behind nginx (Compose, single host)

Topology: proxy (nginx, the only published port) → api (gunicorn, port 5000 on internal networks only) → db (Postgres) and cache (Redis). Two networks keep traffic separated: edge (proxy ↔ api) and backend (api ↔ db/cache, no route outside). The proxy cannot reach the database or cache. Postgres data lives in the named volume db-data; Redis AOF in cache-data.

This is a single-host Compose deployment, not an orchestrator: no HA, no scheduling, no zero-downtime upgrades. Host loss = downtime. Restarts (unless-stopped), log rotation, and CPU/memory limits in compose.yaml are local guard-rails, not a substitute for monitoring or a second host.

Prerequisites and assumptions

  • Docker Engine with Compose v2 (docker compose version).
  • The application exposes GET /health returning HTTP 200 when it can serve (used by the api healthchecks; make it check DB/Redis connectivity with short timeouts so "healthy" is meaningful).
  • The app entrypoint is app.py exposing app (the gunicorn target in the Dockerfile). If yours differs, change the final app:app argument.
  • The app honours DATABASE_URL / REDIS_URL and trusts X-Forwarded-* only from this proxy (e.g. Werkzeug ProxyFix with x_for=1, x_proto=1) — safe because the api port is never published.
  • The api and proxy containers run with read-only filesystems. If the app must write files (uploads, local caches), add a named volume for that path instead of removing read_only.

Configure

cp .env.example .env
# Fill in POSTGRES_PASSWORD and REDIS_PASSWORD, e.g. with: openssl rand -hex 32
chmod 600 .env

Passwords must be URL-safe (hex output is) because Compose embeds them in DATABASE_URL / REDIS_URL. Compose refuses to start if either password is empty — that failure is intentional.

Startup

docker compose config        # validate + show interpolated config (check for surprises)
docker compose build api
docker compose up -d
docker compose ps            # wait until db/cache/api/proxy show "(healthy)"

Startup order is health-gated: api starts only after pg_isready and the authenticated Redis PING pass; the proxy starts only after api /health passes. First boot is slower while Postgres initialises the volume.

Health verification

docker compose ps                          # STATUS column shows health
curl -fsS http://localhost/healthz         # nginx itself: "ok"
curl -fsS http://localhost/health          # through proxy to the API
docker compose logs --tail=100 api db cache proxy
docker compose exec db pg_isready
docker compose exec cache redis-cli --no-auth-warning -a "$REDIS_PASSWORD" ping   # paste value from .env

If a service is unhealthy, read its logs first, then the failing check:

docker inspect --format '{{json .State.Health}}' app-api-1   # adjust container name

Also confirm nothing except the proxy is reachable from the host: docker compose ps should show a published port only for proxy (0.0.0.0:80->80/tcp by default). db/cache/api must show no host ports.

Backup and restore (Postgres)

Back up with a logical dump (safe while running), and store dumps off the host — a backup that only exists next to the volume is not a backup.

# Backup (.env values apply inside the container)
docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U app -d app -Fc > backup-$(date +%F).dump
sha256sum backup-*.dump

# Restore into a running stack (app stopped to avoid writes mid-restore)
docker compose stop api proxy
docker compose exec -T db pg_restore -U app -d app --clean --if-exists < backup-YYYY-MM-DD.dump
docker compose up -d

Rehearse a restore before you need one (e.g. into a scratch docker compose -p restore-test project) and verify the API passes its health checks against the restored data. Never run docker compose down -v on the real project unless data loss is intended — it deletes db-data.

Secret handling

  • .env is the only place secrets live: mode 600, listed in .gitignore and .dockerignore, never pasted into images, tickets, or chat. .env.example ships with empty password fields on purpose.
  • Anyone with Docker/host access can read container env and command lines (docker inspect, ps), including the Redis password. Treat host and Docker-socket access as root-equivalent and restrict it accordingly.
  • Rotation: generate a new value, update .env, then docker compose up -d db cache api to recreate the affected containers. For Postgres, change the password inside the database too (ALTER USER app PASSWORD '...') — POSTGRES_PASSWORD only sets it on first volume initialisation; rotate DB first, then .env, then restart api. Redis picks up the new password on recreate; restart api after it.
  • If a secret ever lands in git or an image, rotate it — deleting the commit/file is not enough.
  • For anything beyond this single-host setup, move secrets to the platform's secret store instead of a file on disk.

Release and rollback

Releases are image tags. Set APP_VERSION in .env per release:

# Release
sed -i 's/^APP_VERSION=.*/APP_VERSION=1.4.0/' .env   # or edit by hand
docker compose build api          # builds app-api:1.4.0
docker compose up -d api proxy    # proxy waits for the new api to be healthy
docker compose ps && curl -fsS http://localhost/health

# Rollback (no rebuild needed while the old image is still on the host)
sed -i 's/^APP_VERSION=.*/APP_VERSION=1.3.2/' .env
docker compose up -d api proxy

Keep the last few app-api:* images on the host; prune only after a release has baked. Database migrations constrain rollback: only roll the app back across backward-compatible migrations; otherwise restore the database from backup (above) and accept the downtime — Compose gives you no rolling deploys, so expect a brief outage while api recreates.

Updating base images

Base images are patch/minor-pinned (python:3.13.7-slim-bookworm, postgres:17.6-alpine, redis:7.4-alpine, nginx:1.27.5-alpine). Bump them deliberately: edit the tag, docker compose build/pull, redeploy, watch health and logs. For stricter reproducibility, pin by digest (image: postgres:17.6-alpine@sha256:... from docker compose images / the registry) once a tested build exists. Similarly, requirements.txt pins exact versions; add hashes (pip install --require-hashes, generated e.g. via pip-tools) if the supply-chain bar rises.

TLS

This stack serves plain HTTP on HTTP_PORT. For real users, terminate TLS in front (host reverse proxy or load balancer) or add certificates to nginx.conf (and then enable HSTS). X-Forwarded-Proto already reflects the scheme nginx saw, so app-level secure-cookie/redirect logic keeps working either way.

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/TASK.md

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.

# Copy to .env, fill in the secrets, then: chmod 600 .env
# Never commit .env (it is listed in .gitignore and .dockerignore).
#
# Generate each password with a URL-safe alphabet, because the values are
# embedded into DATABASE_URL / REDIS_URL by compose.yaml:
#   openssl rand -hex 32

# Host port the proxy publishes. Keep 80 unless something else owns it.

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/.gitignore

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/compose.yaml

# Small single-host deployment: nginx -> Python API -> Postgres / Redis.
#
# All secrets come from a local .env file (cp .env.example .env, then fill
# it in and `chmod 600 .env`). Values marked `:?` make Compose fail fast
# with a clear error instead of silently starting with an empty password.
# Nothing credential-shaped is committed in this file.
#
# Compose is a single-host process manager here, not an orchestrator: no
# scheduling, no HA, no zero-downtime deploys. restart/log/limit settings
# below are local guard-rails only (see RUNBOOK.md).

name: app

x-logging: &default-logging
  driver: json-file
  options:
    max-size: "10m"
    max-file: "3"

services:
  proxy:
    image: nginx:1.27.5-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    # The only published port in the stack. Terminate TLS in front of this
    # (host reverse proxy / load balancer) for real production traffic, or
    # extend nginx.conf with certificates -- do not serve users over plain
    # HTTP on the open internet.
    ports:
      - "${HTTP_PORT:-80}:80"
    volumes:
      - ./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/nginx.conf:ro
    depends_on:
      api:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - edge
    read_only: true
    tmpfs:
      - /var/cache/nginx
      - /var/run
      - /tmp
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    healthcheck:
      # /healthz is answered by nginx itself (see nginx.conf); busybox wget
      # ships in the Alpine image.
      test: ["CMD", "wget", "-q", "-O", "/dev/null", "http://127.0.0.1/healthz"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 3s
      start_period: 5s
      retries: 3
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          cpus: "0.50"
          memory: 128M

  api:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
    # Tagging the built image makes rollback a version change, not a rebuild
    # (APP_VERSION in .env, bumped per release; see RUNBOOK.md).
    image: app-api:${APP_VERSION:-dev}
    restart: unless-stopped
    environment:
      APP_ENV: production
      # Built from .env values; passwords must be URL-safe (see .env.example).
      DATABASE_URL: postgresql://${POSTGRES_USER:-app}:${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}@db:5432/${POSTGRES_DB:-app}
      REDIS_URL: redis://:${REDIS_PASSWORD:?set REDIS_PASSWORD in .env}@cache:6379/0
    # Internal visibility only: reachable from proxy via the edge network,
    # never published to the host.
    expose:
      - "5000"
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      cache:
        condition: service_healthy
    networks:
      - edge
      - backend
    read_only: true
    tmpfs:
      - /tmp
    cap_drop:
      - ALL
    security_opt:
      - no-new-privileges:true
    stop_grace_period: 30s
    healthcheck:
      # Mirrors the Dockerfile HEALTHCHECK; drives proxy startup ordering.
      test: ["CMD", "python", "-c", "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:5000/health', timeout=3)"]
      interval: 15s
      timeout: 5s
      start_period: 20s
      retries: 3
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          cpus: "1.00"
          memory: 512M

  db:
    image: postgres:17.6-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: ${POSTGRES_USER:-app}
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?set POSTGRES_PASSWORD in .env}
      POSTGRES_DB: ${POSTGRES_DB:-app}
    # No ports: only the api can reach Postgres, over the backend network.
    volumes:
      - db-data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    networks:
      - backend
    stop_grace_period: 30s
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U $${POSTGRES_USER:-app} -d $${POSTGRES_DB:-app}"]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 5s
      start_period: 10s
      retries: 5
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          cpus: "1.00"
          memory: 512M

  cache:
    image: redis:7.4-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    # Password auth as defence in depth on top of network isolation.
    # (Compose interpolation means the value is visible via `docker inspect`
    # to anyone with Docker access on the host -- treat host/Docker access
    # as privileged; see RUNBOOK.md.)
    command: ["redis-server", "--appendonly", "yes", "--requirepass", "${REDIS_PASSWORD:?set REDIS_PASSWORD in .env}"]
    environment:
      REDIS_PASSWORD: ${REDIS_PASSWORD:?set REDIS_PASSWORD in .env}
    # No ports: only the api can reach Redis, over the backend network.
    volumes:
      - cache-data:/data
    networks:
      - backend
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "redis-cli --no-auth-warning -a \"$${REDIS_PASSWORD}\" ping | grep -q PONG"]
      interval: 10s
      timeout: 3s
      start_period: 5s
      retries: 5
    logging: *default-logging
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          cpus: "0.50"
          memory: 256M

# Two explicit networks: proxy <-> api (edge) and api <-> db/cache
# (backend, internal = no route to the outside world). The proxy cannot
# reach the database or cache at all, even if it is compromised.
networks:
  edge:
    driver: bridge
  backend:
    driver: bridge
    internal: true

volumes:
  # Survives `docker compose down`; only `down -v` destroys data (see the
  # backup/restore section of RUNBOOK.md before ever running that).
  db-data:
    driver: local
  # Redis AOF. If Redis is ever reduced to a pure throwaway cache this
  # volume (and --appendonly) can be dropped.
  cache-data:
    driver: local

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/Dockerfile

# Pin the patch version and slim variant: reproducible base, small surface.
# Bump deliberately after testing; do not use floating tags such as `python:3.13`.
FROM python:3.13.7-slim-bookworm

ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
    PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1 \
    PIP_DISABLE_PIP_VERSION_CHECK=1

WORKDIR /app

# Dedicated non-root user/group to run the application.
RUN groupadd --system app \
    && useradd --system --gid app --no-create-home app

# Install dependencies before copying source so this layer is cached until
# requirements.txt changes. Exact versions come from the pinned
# requirements.txt (Flask, gunicorn, psycopg2-binary, redis) -- never from a
# floating `pip install flask ...` line.
COPY requirements.txt ./
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Copy application source. .dockerignore keeps .env and other local files
# out of the image.
COPY --chown=app:app . .

USER app

EXPOSE 5000

# The application must expose GET /health returning HTTP 200 when it can
# serve traffic (see RUNBOOK.md). Uses only the Python standard library so
# no curl/wget has to be installed into the slim image.
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=15s --retries=3 \
    CMD python -c "import urllib.request; urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:5000/health', timeout=3)"

# Production WSGI server (never `flask run`). Assumes the app module is
# `app.py` exposing `app` -- adjust the final argument (e.g. `wsgi:app`)
# if the project uses a different entrypoint. Tune workers/threads to the
# host (roughly 2 x vCPU workers is a common starting point).
CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:5000", "--workers", "2", "--threads", "4", \
     "--timeout", "30", "--graceful-timeout", "30", \
     "--access-logfile", "-", "--error-logfile", "-", "app:app"]

muse-wonder-full/03-infra-management/nginx.conf

# Reverse proxy for the JSON API: proxy -> api:5000 (Compose `edge` network).

user nginx;
worker_processes auto;
error_log /var/log/nginx/error.log warn;
pid /tmp/nginx.pid;

events {
    worker_connections 1024;
}

http {
    include /etc/nginx/mime.types;
    default_type application/octet-stream;

    # Logs go to the container's stdout/stderr (the image symlinks these
    # paths), where Compose's bounded json-file logging captures them.
    access_log /var/log/nginx/access.log combined;

    server_tokens off;
    sendfile on;
    keepalive_timeout 65s;

    # Temp paths under tmpfs mounts so the container can run read-only.
    client_body_temp_path /tmp/client_body;
    proxy_temp_path /tmp/proxy;
    fastcgi_temp_path /tmp/fastcgi;
    uwsgi_temp_path /tmp/uwsgi;
    scgi_temp_path /tmp/scgi;

    # JSON compresses well; only bother above 1 KiB.
    gzip on;
    gzip_types application/json;
    gzip_min_length 1024;

    # Reject oversized JSON payloads before they reach the API. Raise this
    # only for specific endpoints that legitimately need larger bodies.
    client_max_body_size 1m;
    client_body_timeout 10s;
    client_header_timeout 10s;
    send_timeout 30s;

    # Docker's embedded DNS. Combined with the variable in proxy_pass below
    # this makes nginx re-resolve `api` at runtime, so a recreated api
    # container (new IP) does not require an nginx restart and a stale IP
    # cannot black-hole traffic.
    resolver 127.0.0.11 valid=30s ipv6=off;
    resolver_timeout 5s;

    server {
        listen 80;
        server_name _;

        # Conservative headers for an API (clients are programs, not
        # browsers rendering pages; HSTS only makes sense once TLS is on).
        add_header X-Content-Type-Options "nosniff" always;
        add_header X-Frame-Options "DENY" always;
        add_header Referrer-Policy "no-referrer" always;

        # Liveness of nginx itself, used by the Compose healthcheck.
        location = /healthz {
            access_log off;
            default_type text/plain;
            return 200 "ok\n";
        }

        location / {
            # Variable (not a literal host) so the resolver above is used
            # per-request instead of resolving once at startup.
            set $api_upstream api;
            proxy_pass http://$api_upstream:5000;
            proxy_http_version 1.1;

            # Tell the API who the real client is. The app must only trust
            # these headers because the api port is not published (only
            # this proxy can reach it). Enable the framework's proxy-fix
            # handling (e.g. Werkzeug ProxyFix, x_for=1, x_proto=1).
            proxy_set_header Host $host;
            proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
            proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
            proxy_set_header Connection "";

            # Fail fast instead of stacking up hung requests: connect
            # quickly, and give the API at most one gunicorn worker-cycle
            # (its --timeout is 30s) plus margin to answer.
            proxy_connect_timeout 5s;
            proxy_send_timeout 30s;
            proxy_read_timeout 60s;
        }
    }
}

Flask==3.1.2

psycopg2-binary==2.9.10

Evaluation 7.5 / 10 graded blind as submission H

Good structure: variable proxy_pass for DNS re-resolution, read_only api, image tagging for rollback, and a detailed runbook. Redis is unauthenticated, though it sits on the internal network. The runbook wrongly says unhealthy services are auto-restarted, and the gunicorn target wsgi:app is a guess.

Strengths

  • Every credential has a :? guard; backend is internal; only the proxy publishes
  • resolver + variable proxy_pass; 5s/30s timeouts; full forwarded headers
  • IMAGE_TAG-based rollback with --no-build; backup verification with pg_restore --list

Weaknesses

  • RUNBOOK: 'Unhealthy services are auto-restarted (restart: unless-stopped)', which is false
  • 'docker compose restart api # gunicorn graceful reload' is misleading
  • Bind is 0.0.0.0:80; pg_isready over the socket
Evidence the grader checked
  • docker compose config: exit 0; cache has no requirepass in the rendered command
  • RUNBOOK.md Health verification paragraph on auto-restart

qwen3.8-max/03-infra-management/RESPONSE.md

RESPONSE — review of the original deployment configs

Highest-risk problems in the original files (ranked)

  1. Postgres and Redis published on host ports with hardcoded credentials (compose.yaml: admin:password inline, 5432:5432, 6379:6379, plus 5000:5000 for the API). On any host with a public interface this exposes the database to the internet behind a guessable password — direct data breach / ransom risk. Redis was exposed with no auth at all. Fix: all credentials now come from .env (${VAR:?err} interpolation, fails loudly when missing); db/cache publish no ports and sit on an internal: true network; the API is reachable only via nginx; the single published port is the proxy's.

  2. Development Flask server as PID 1, as root (Dockerfile: FLASK_ENV=development, CMD flask run). The Werkzeug dev server is single-threaded, not hardened, and in debug mode exposes an interactive Python console on error pages — remote code execution if reachable; any exploit also lands as root in the container. Fix: gunicorn (already pinned in requirements.txt) with bounded workers/threads/timeouts, and a dedicated non-root user (app, UID/GID 10001); compose adds no-new-privileges, read_only rootfs + /tmp tmpfs, and init.

  3. No Postgres volume — the database lived in the container's writable layer, so any recreate (up -d, image update, OOM kill) destroyed all data. Fix: named volume pgdata (plus redisdata for Redis AOF) and a documented backup/restore procedure in RUNBOOK.md.

  4. Non-reproducible builds (FROM python:3.13 full image, pip install flask psycopg2 redis unpinned, ignoring the pinned requirements.txt; nginx/postgres/redis:latest in compose). Deploys drifted silently and pulled in unvetted versions (supply-chain + "works on Tuesday" risk); plain psycopg2 also implies an unnecessary compiler in the image. Fix: python:3.13-slim, install strictly from requirements.txt (copied first for layer caching), base images pinned to minor versions (nginx:1.28-alpine, postgres:17-alpine, redis:7.4-alpine).

  5. No health checks, depends_on without conditions — containers started in name-only order, so the API raced Postgres/Redis at boot and nginx happily 502'd; failures were silent. Fix: health checks on all four services (app /healthz, /nginx-health, pg_isready, redis-cli ping) and depends_on: condition: service_healthy chaining proxy -> api -> db/cache.

  6. nginx proxy missing forwarding headers and timeouts — the app saw the proxy's IP as client IP, wrong scheme, and a hung backend could pin connections forever; the hostname api was resolved once at boot, so recreating the API container left nginx pointing at a dead IP until manual restart. Fix: X-Forwarded-For/Proto/Host, X-Real-IP, proxy_http_version 1.1, connect/send/read timeouts (5s/30s/30s), client_max_body_size 1m, gzip for JSON, server_tokens off, and runtime re-resolution via Docker DNS (resolver 127.0.0.11 + variable proxy_pass).

  7. COPY . . with no .dockerignore — a local .env, git history, and caches would be baked into image layers (secret leakage via image sharing) and invalidate the dependency layer on every code change. Fix: .dockerignore added; requirements copied/installed before code.

  8. No restart policy, log rotation, or resource limits — a crashed service stayed dead, json logs grew unbounded (disk exhaustion), and one runaway container could starve the host. Fix: restart: unless-stopped, json-file logs capped at 3x10 MB per service, per-service CPU/memory limits via deploy.resources.limits — explicitly as guardrails, with RUNBOOK notes that Compose offers no orchestration beyond this.

Verification performed (no containers started, no network used)

  • docker compose config (Compose v5.5.0) with dummy env vars: passes; rendered output confirms only proxy publishes a port (80), health checks and depends_on: service_healthy conditions survive rendering, resource limits/log options/internal network are present, and DATABASE_URL interpolates correctly from the environment.
  • docker compose config without env vars: fails fast with clear "required variable ... is missing" errors — confirms no silent default credentials.
  • Python checks over the rendered/final files: YAML parses; db, cache, and api define no ports:; backend network is internal: true; pgdata/redisdata volumes declared; no admin:password-style literals remain in any committed file.
  • Static lint of nginx.conf: balanced braces/quotes; forwarding headers, timeouts, resolver, variable-based proxy_pass, server_tokens off, and both health locations present. nginx -t could not be run (would require pulling/starting a container, which the task forbids); the config uses the variable-upstream pattern that also passes nginx -t outside Docker because no name is resolved at config-load time.
  • Static lint of Dockerfile: slim base, dependency layer before code copy, non-root USER before CMD/HEALTHCHECK, no FLASK_ENV, gunicorn exec- form CMD, exec-form health check.
  • Image build (docker build) was not run: it requires network access to pull bases/packages, which is out of scope per the task constraints.

Assumptions and residual risks

  • The app source is not in this directory. Dockerfile assumes WSGI entry point wsgi:app and a GET /healthz endpoint (should verify DB/Redis); adjust the CMD if the module differs. requirements.txt was already pinned and was kept as-is.
  • Internal API port changed 5000 -> 8000 (gunicorn convention); only nginx talks to it, nothing external changes.
  • No TLS: nginx listens on 80 and expects an upstream terminator (or added certs) before real internet exposure.
  • Minor-version pins, not digests; for full reproducibility pin digests and add --require-hashes to requirements in CI.
  • read_only: true on api assumes the app writes only to /tmp.

qwen3.8-max/03-infra-management/RUNBOOK.md

RUNBOOK — small-api (nginx + Flask API + Postgres + Redis)

Single-host Docker Compose deployment. Not Kubernetes, not multi-node.

Architecture

internet -> host:${HTTP_PORT:-80} -> proxy (nginx, "frontend" net)
                                       -> api (gunicorn :8000, frontend+backend)
                                            -> db    (postgres:17, backend only, internal)
                                            -> cache (redis:7.4,   backend only, internal)
  • The only published port is the proxy's HTTP port. api, db, and cache have no host ports; db/cache sit on an internal: true network.
  • Data volumes: pgdata (Postgres), redisdata (Redis AOF). Redis is configured as a cache (allkeys-lru, 256 MB); Postgres is the source of truth and the only thing that must be backed up.
  • The API image expects a WSGI entry point wsgi:app (adjust the CMD in Dockerfile if yours is e.g. app:app) and a GET /healthz endpoint returning 200 (it should verify DB/Redis connectivity).

Prerequisites

  • Docker Engine 24+ with Compose v2 (docker compose version).
  • Free port 80 on the host (or set HTTP_PORT in .env).

Startup

cp .env.example .env
chmod 600 .env
# edit .env: real POSTGRES_PASSWORD (openssl rand -hex 32), etc.

docker compose up -d --build
docker compose ps          # wait until all services show "healthy"

Startup order is enforced by health-gated depends_on: db/cache healthy -> api healthy -> proxy starts. Compose exits with a clear error if .env is missing required variables.

Health verification

docker compose ps                                   # HEALTH column = healthy
curl -fsS http://localhost:${HTTP_PORT:-80}/healthz # end-to-end (nginx -> api -> deps)
curl -fsS http://localhost:${HTTP_PORT:-80}/nginx-health  # proxy alone

docker compose exec db    pg_isready -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB"
docker compose exec cache redis-cli ping            # -> PONG

# Inspect raw health probe history for a service:
docker inspect --format '{{json .State.Health}}' \
  $(docker compose ps -q api) | python3 -m json.tool

Unhealthy services are auto-restarted (restart: unless-stopped); check docker compose logs -f <svc> and docker inspect ... .State.Health for the failing probe output.

Backup / restore (Postgres)

Logical dump — safe while running; take before every deploy and on a cron (e.g. nightly, keep 14 days off-host):

mkdir -p backups
docker compose exec -T db sh -c \
  'pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -Fc' \
  > "backups/app-$(date +%F-%H%M).dump"

# verify the dump is readable:
pg_restore --list "backups/app-YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.dump" | head

Restore (destructive — drops and recreates objects):

docker compose up -d db                 # ensure db is healthy first
docker compose exec -T db sh -c \
  'pg_restore -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" --clean --if-exists' \
  < backups/app-YYYY-MM-DD-HHMM.dump
docker compose restart api              # clear stale connections/pools

Cold volume backup (rare; full-fidelity, requires downtime):

docker compose stop db
docker run --rm -v small-api_pgdata:/data -v "$PWD/backups":/out alpine \
  tar czf /out/pgdata-$(date +%F).tgz -C /data .
docker compose start db

Redis holds cache only: do not back it up; it repopulates on its own.

Secret handling

  • All secrets live in .env (mode 600, git-ignored, never in images or compose.yaml). .env.example contains placeholders only.
  • Generate: openssl rand -hex 32. Keep POSTGRES_PASSWORD alphanumeric or URL-encode it — it is interpolated into DATABASE_URL.
  • Rotate the DB password:
    1. docker compose exec db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -c "ALTER USER \"$POSTGRES_USER\" PASSWORD 'NEW-PASSWORD';"
    2. Update .env, then docker compose up -d api (recreates api with the new URL).
  • If .env is ever leaked or committed, treat all values as compromised: rotate immediately and scrub git history.
  • Scaling up: move to Docker secrets / an external secret manager and mount secrets as files instead of env vars; env vars are visible via docker inspect to anyone with docker access.

Rollback

Every deploy should tag the built API image so you can step back:

# at deploy time (release v1.4.0):
docker compose build api
docker tag small-api:local small-api:v1.4.0
IMAGE_TAG=v1.4.0 docker compose up -d        # runs the tagged image

Roll back the application:

IMAGE_TAG=v1.3.0 docker compose up -d --no-build api   # previous known-good tag
docker compose ps && curl -fsS http://localhost/healthz

Roll back configuration (compose.yaml / nginx.conf / Dockerfile): revert the commit in git, then docker compose up -d (nginx-only changes: docker compose exec proxy nginx -s reload after re-rendering, or docker compose restart proxy).

Database: schema migrations are not auto-reversible. If a deploy included migrations, roll the data back by restoring the pre-deploy dump (above) — take that dump before deploying. Prefer backward-compatible migrations (expand/contract) so old app versions run against the new schema.

Never run docker compose down -v on a live host — it deletes pgdata.

Routine operations

docker compose logs -f api          # logs are json-file, rotated 3x10 MB
docker compose restart api          # gunicorn graceful reload
docker compose down                 # stop (volumes survive)
docker compose pull && docker compose up -d   # patch base images (test first)

Resource limits (CPU/memory per service) are set in compose.yaml under deploy.resources.limits — tune to the host, they are guardrails, not autoscaling. Compose gives you no leader election, no zero-downtime rolling updates, and no self-healing beyond container restarts; plan a maintenance window for deploys and keep an eye on df -h for the docker data root.

Known caveats / assumptions

  • TLS terminates upstream (cloud LB, reverse proxy, or add 443 + certs to nginx). Port 80 is plain HTTP; do not expose this directly to the internet without TLS in front.
  • api runs with read_only: true and a writable /tmp tmpfs; if the app must write elsewhere, add a named volume for that path.
  • Image tags are pinned to minor versions (nginx 1.28, postgres 17, redis 7.4, python 3.13-slim); for full reproducibility pin digests in CI and add hashes to requirements.txt (pip-compile --generate-hashes).

qwen3.8-max/03-infra-management/TASK.md

Task 03 — Infrastructure management: harden a small deployment

Review and improve the supplied Dockerfile, compose.yaml, and nginx.conf for a small Python API behind nginx with Postgres and Redis. Do not add Kubernetes or unrelated services.

Goals:

  • No embedded credentials or public database/cache ports.
  • The API runs as a non-root user with a production server and reproducible dependency installation.
  • Add meaningful health checks and startup dependencies based on health.
  • Persist Postgres data, make internal service communication explicit, and add sensible restart/logging/resource settings without pretending Compose is a full orchestrator.
  • Proxy headers and timeouts should be appropriate for a JSON API.
  • Keep local operation straightforward through .env.example.

Deliver corrected configuration and a concise RUNBOOK.md covering startup, health verification, backup/restore, secret handling, and rollback. Write RESPONSE.md listing the highest-risk original problems and verification you performed. Work only in this directory; do not actually start containers.
